Tips and Tools You are asking the right questions and seeing the right things. I think the answer has to do with what your expectations are. We "expect" a reaction of some sort at Supply 1 so taking a trade there makes sense. But, you shouldn't then "expect" it to just cruise through demand. And, as you said, when it didn't at least engulf that new demand, it's better to take some or all of your profit. This is something I'm still working on. If you look at my journal you'll see that I took a similar EU short today but didn't take my profit and ended up with a BE. Also, note on your original trade, you didn't take the 1st visit back to the supply but the 3rd visit. This is something that I had a hard time with since it's nice to see some confirmation that price is reacting where you expect it to. This is where dropping down into the LTF and getting in on a retracement after the initial reaction makes sense. With Regards KGB https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=225 15/31 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 10 - RTM Community HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT Attachments: QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE THANK YOU In2forex Journal #240 in2forex Offline Associate Trader Posts: 357 Thank you received: 711 Hi KBG Very valid question, it shows me that you are really looking at the market in total. Concentrating on entries and exits are both as important as the other, the only difference for me is that when in a trade different aspects of emotions start to play on your mind. Learning to control these, is as important as choosing a good entry or exit. Enough rambling. To answer your question, i'm going to run through the process I went through in making the trade As I have mentioned previously in my journal, I have had to change the way I trade due to other commitments which have reduced my chart time to almost zero. This trade was based on a total of about 5 minutes of screen time. 16/31 https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=225 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 10 - RTM Community Entry: On Friday morning I open about 5 pairs of charts all HTF and flicked through HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT them looking for something to jump out at me. On the GBPJPY I saw the Cap and compression (A good looking can can in development). I drew in some rectangles noticing the ENG (1) and the FL(3) set my PO at that level and my stop loss above a higher FTR which you can see in the LTF). At this point I was only interested in a reaction and price falling through the CP. I didn't set a TP. I thought I'll check on the trade later during the day just before NFP new which was due out at about 14h30 my time. As it worked out I never got a chance to look at the chart again until after the market had closed on Friday night. When I did open the chart I notice the PO had been triggered and the reaction to my entry was perfect, price had reached the FTR you mention in your chart. I would have loved to exit then and I was kicking myself for not setting a TP. I couldn't do anything as the market was closed. Exit:: I thought of waiting for the market to open and then exit the trade but this was a demo trade (sleep was a reasonable priority) so I thought let me let it run. When I woke up I check the chart, price had engulfed up. I then did something I try avoid, I looked at the trade profit and that was a good enough reason to exit considering I wasn't able to manage the trade on a real time bases. So in conclusion the exit of this trade was based on emotion and not PA. In the past my general strategy has been to exit a trade when I'm confident to take a trade in the opposite direction based on PA. Due to my current screen time limitations I can't do this now. So I'm having to change how I trade. This is just another learning curve on the journey to success. . I'm still struggling with the emotional stress when in a trade and often find myself looking at the profit, hence why I'm trying to avoid this. Sorry for the long post, hope it helped. Now I need to get back to work. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT Amazing. What If reveals is that some of his best students are defining the entry and then taking off the majority at TG1. Letting small runners go if the market wants to get to TG2, TG3, etc. Anyway, I am still demo trading but this is how I trade now. I take off 75% or 67% at TG1 and let the runners go, moving the stop to a likely pain free zone (opposite side of a nice FTR for example, but still in profit. Not focusing on a problem directly moves it from conscious to subconscious and that can be a more powerful and creative tool for problem solving. Scientists have even proven (finally) that walking helps problem solving - the issue at hand is there, but it's not prioritised - the chains are off that wonderful subconscious... . It seems that trading is full of stumbling blocks, no matter how far along the road you think you are! Matters aren't helped by the fact that it's based on probabilities - you can be right and lose money, and you can be wrong but make money. The contrary feedback from these two entirely different scenarios doesn't help you when you are trying to learn what is right-thinking and what is wrong-thinking. Even if you spend many hours investigating Price Action on the left (an absolute must), it doesn't necessarily convert into success at the hard right edge. But it is definitely one https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 14/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community of the building blocks of success. If you're having trouble at the hard right edge now it HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT might pay you to go back to the left. I don't know how you think through or solve problems, but it's usually best to identify them, state them, and break them down bite sized chunks to be solved. You will have noticed that this is a major part of how I approach things - it's got me this far, but I really am struggling with the next bit right now . I also don't know what draws your attention when you look at a chart: if several people look at the same chart, they will all see different things! For instance: 1. Do you notice the peaks and troughs as price zigs and zags or do you just notice the general direction that price takes over time? 2. Do you notice horizontal levels where price reacts or do you notice diagonal lines? 3. Do you notice spikes from levels or clusters at levels? Knowing what you automatically key in to might be the first step in working through your stumbling block. When reading others' journals remember that although everyone is pretty much doing the same thing here, they may describe it differently, and each may arrive at the same point by different paths: that is a reflection of those different thought processes we each have. You might find one person's way confusing because you just don't see things their way and you might gel with another's because you do! It might be that as your experience and knowledge grow the first person's way becomes more obvious! There is no right or wrong to this (all views are valid if they are long-term profitable) you just have to find and stick with what works for you and grow from there - another of those building blocks for success. If you don't know what you key in to try what I did way way back. I took charts and drew 'random' lines on them (knowing I would necessarily apply some element of judgment: brains aren't good at random, they are pattern seekers). I looked at what converged on those lines. I rinsed and repeated this several times until I confirmed that I was generally identifying the same things each time. Then I built from that. Hopefully there's something of use here, but good luck and good hunting. This is my take on it so it probably isn't "according to Hoyle", keep your pinches of salt to hand Compression usually happens at the end of a Momentum Move (or some sort of main move). Buyers or sellers have thrust price - unopposed - in their chosen direction https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 25/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community (either because they had the strength to overwhelm the opposition or the opposition HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT was sorely lacking (each can have different implications)). Eventually they reach an area where opposition slowly increases - the Momentum Move is over but inertia (read late buying or selling) sees price continue in the same direction. The strength is slowly sapped out of this incursive move until there is none left and the opposition can now push price in their direction... . Compression should be self-defeating, that's why it's thought of as a leading 'indicator' as price approaches Supply or Demand. What you see is the law of diminishing returns in effect: each successive thrust depletes orders to support the move, sees less distance covered for the same effort, and drives price deeper into opposition, perhaps even convinces opposition they are witnessing increasing weakness so attracting more of the same. The very shape (wedge-like) expresses Compression! But it is not really Compression (in the sense it is meant in RTM) until price can close within its confines - that is, we do not know whether it is consumption of orders or rejection of 'cheap' prices, until we know . That is, price could just as easily 'compress' into Supply or Demand and then drive straight through it (there are many variables at play here). Also, that price is likely to be turned back at the last place of strength (i.e. the transition from Momo to Compression) implies the beginning of ranging and perhaps a continuation of price in the same direction as Momo, but as with all things it doesn't have to be this way. Compression examples (please note that a little poetic licence was used ): Warning: Spoiler! [ Click to expand ] What you were seeing on EURUSD is a Flag. There are some similarities and many differences between a Flag and Compression: 1. Both occur at the end of Momo (or some form of main move) https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 26/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community 2. Compression continues with the Momo whereas the Flag goes against it, HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT because... 3. Compression occurs going into opposition Supply or Demand whereas a Flag occurs after hitting it 4. This also means that when Compression eventually hits Supply or Demand it is still fresh whereas the Flag has already started depleting it, so... 5. On retracing through Compression price is likely to be turned back at the last place where strength was shown whereas once a Flag has completed its pull back (to a source from which the Momo or main move can continue) it is likely to drive through its own extreme and continue with the main move 6. But, the transition from Momo to Compression is a base of strength which might lead to support ranging, and that might lead to a continuation of the main move, so in the end the outcome might be the same for both Compression and Flag even though the mechanism is different 7. Both deplete orders within their structures, removing obstacles to any move through them 8. Oh, and Compression is generally wedge-shaped whereas Flags form equidistant channels EURUSD Flag: Warning: Spoiler! [ Click to expand ] Remember: learner driver, so caveat lector! But I hope this helps all the same. Regards. Spongy. I post the following chart to demonstrate my understanding. https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 29/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT My main challenge with compression is that very often what looks like compression to me on the HTF doesn't translate to compression in the LTF. The resent PA from 8 November to 19 November on the EURUSD is an example of this. I suppose our own understanding of compression may determine how we read price and trade it. As long as this is profitable then great! Sorry about the spelling error in the chart "toughed" should read "touched" Attachments: https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 30/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community The following user(s) said Thank You: Spongiform, Michal238 LOGOUT HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE Moving from thinking to doing QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE THANK YOU In2forex Journal #46 Spongiform Offline Trader Posts: 196 Thank you received: 273 Hello in2forex, Unfortunately there always seems to be fun when it comes to interpreting patterns, or even naming them. Mine is my way based on what I saw in the Compression Homework section when I first visited, reinforced by my many visits to Redsword11's PIE forum on Forex Factory (950 pages and still going...). I haven't been into the Compression Homework section for a long while but I noticed there is now a PDF at its start, it's instructional :) Compression.pdf There are several examples that agree with your definition, but there's one specific one (on page 19, showing both structures on one chart, but you might want to start reading the text from page 16 as well) which highlights all the difference for me - it's from Redsword11. https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 31/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community As I say, there are several similarities between Compression and Flags, not least that HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT as each rises to Supply (or falls to Demand) it absorbs buy (sell) orders, creating weakness within its structure, allowing price to move through it with relative ease (if that is the direction that price then takes). But there are important differences which prepare us to anticipate different Price Action after we see them: after Compression price might range in a PAZ before making its next move (possibly continuing in the same direction as the main move); after a Flag price might just shoot through and continue on in what's called a measured move (the first 'CP' example in your chart above shows this, so does Redsword11's example on page 19). It's a shame that Redsword11's example doesn't capture what happened after the CP, but there are other examples from page 20 onwards which not only blur the definition of Compression but show price ranging after it. None of these things is set in concrete, and anything can and will happen - it's all about the next candle! And, of course, reference time-frame is important, so while your H1 example shows two Flags (as I would call them), they are part of a larger structure on H4 and D1 (in this case a range). It's sometimes difficult to decide what you're seeing and what you should pay most attention to... . In the end, as long as you know what you mean by Compression, and what you anticipate could happen after, that's what matters most. :) Putting all that to one side, I'm not entirely sure I understand what you mean when you say your "main challenge is very often what looks like compression to me on the HTF doesn't translate to compression in the LTF". If I've understood the challenge correctly (and I'm probably very wide of the mark), then if you see Compression on some HTF (spiking into Demand as price rises, for instance), all you do on LTF is check whether ever higher Demand areas are being created in the shadows of those HTF spikes and that all these stacked Demands have been tested. There will be a lot of other detail down here, but your focus should be on the structure you saw on HTF. So: Warning: Spoiler! Harry's reply in "about compression" post 851 The following user(s) said Thank You: Michal238 Moving from thinking to doing QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE THANK YOU In2forex Journal #48 Posts: 196 Spongiform Offline Trader 34/38 Thank you received: 273 https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community Hello in2forex, EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT HOME One thing I've learned as time passes, is don't sweat the detail too much (a terrible thing for an engineer to contemplate, let alone say). Price Action is rarely going to be perfect, so if you have a directional bias and you see a road block, that will be your TP1. If you can't get good R : R to this from your chosen opportunity to enter, then it's failed your quality filters - drop it and move on. The occasional fresh Demand is an occupational hazard of Compression, and we don't know whether it will be respected or disregarded when price gets there. For the EURUSD example - if you could have found an area to get in with good R : R - that fresh Demand level was an obvious point for TP1. When I saw that particular Demand area print I thought it would be a great area to get long at (which is why it was an obvious TP1 if you were looking to short), but because it was in a Flag I was only thinking of shorts. When I step back and take in the D1, that whole structure is not much in the grand scheme of things - of course it was going to go up... . Regards. Spongy. The following user(s) said Thank You: Michal238 Moving from thinking to doing QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE THANK YOU In2forex Journal #12 DharmaTrader Offline Trader https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal 15/39 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - RTM Community Posts: 180 Thank you received: 327 HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT Start Here in2forex wrote: In2forex, A note before you begin Markepedia I do have a question, If you had taken the trade on the first touch of supply 1 Markepedia Roadmap would you have exited the trade when it failed to move below demand 2, or Abbreviations would you not have taken the trade at all as it hadn't engulfed the fresh demand. Tips and Tools You are asking the right questions and seeing the right things. I think the answer has to do with what your expectations are. We "expect" a reaction of some sort at Supply 1 so taking a trade there makes sense. But, you shouldn't then "expect" it to just cruise through demand. And, as you said, when it didn't at least engulf that new demand, it's better to take some or all of your profit. This is something I'm still working on. If you look at my journal you'll see that I took a similar EU short today but didn't take my profit and ended up with a BE. Also, note on your original trade, you didn't take the 1st visit back to the supply but the 3rd visit. This is something that I had a hard time with since it's nice to see some confirmation that price is reacting where you expect it to. This is where dropping down into the LTF and getting in on a retracement after the initial reaction makes sense. With Regards KGB https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=225 15/31 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 10 - RTM Community HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT Attachments: QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE THANK YOU In2forex Journal #240 in2forex Offline Associate Trader Posts: 357 Thank you received: 711 Hi KBG Very valid question, it shows me that you are really looking at the market in total. Concentrating on entries and exits are both as important as the other, the only difference for me is that when in a trade different aspects of emotions start to play on your mind. Learning to control these, is as important as choosing a good entry or exit. Enough rambling. To answer your question, i'm going to run through the process I went through in making the trade As I have mentioned previously in my journal, I have had to change the way I trade due to other commitments which have reduced my chart time to almost zero. This trade was based on a total of about 5 minutes of screen time. 16/31 https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=225 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 10 - RTM Community Entry: On Friday morning I open about 5 pairs of charts all HTF and flicked through HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT them looking for something to jump out at me. On the GBPJPY I saw the Cap and compression (A good looking can can in development). I drew in some rectangles noticing the ENG (1) and the FL(3) set my PO at that level and my stop loss above a higher FTR which you can see in the LTF). At this point I was only interested in a reaction and price falling through the CP. I didn't set a TP. I thought I'll check on the trade later during the day just before NFP new which was due out at about 14h30 my time. As it worked out I never got a chance to look at the chart again until after the market had closed on Friday night. When I did open the chart I notice the PO had been triggered and the reaction to my entry was perfect, price had reached the FTR you mention in your chart. I would have loved to exit then and I was kicking myself for not setting a TP. I couldn't do anything as the market was closed. Exit:: I thought of waiting for the market to open and then exit the trade but this was a demo trade (sleep was a reasonable priority) so I thought let me let it run. When I woke up I check the chart, price had engulfed up. I then did something I try avoid, I looked at the trade profit and that was a good enough reason to exit considering I wasn't able to manage the trade on a real time bases. So in conclusion the exit of this trade was based on emotion and not PA. In the past my general strategy has been to exit a trade when I'm confident to take a trade in the opposite direction based on PA. Due to my current screen time limitations I can't do this now. So I'm having to change how I trade. This is just another learning curve on the journey to success. . I'm still struggling with the emotional stress when in a trade and often find myself looking at the profit, hence why I'm trying to avoid this. Sorry for the long post, hope it helped. Now I need to get back to work. Not focusing on a problem directly moves it from conscious to subconscious and that can be a more powerful and creative tool for problem solving. Scientists have even proven (finally) that walking helps problem solving - the issue at hand is there, but it's not prioritised - the chains are off that wonderful subconscious... . It seems that trading is full of stumbling blocks, no matter how far along the road you think you are! Matters aren't helped by the fact that it's based on probabilities - you can be right and lose money, and you can be wrong but make money. The contrary feedback from these two entirely different scenarios doesn't help you when you are trying to learn what is right-thinking and what is wrong-thinking. Even if you spend many hours investigating Price Action on the left (an absolute must), it doesn't necessarily convert into success at the hard right edge. But it is definitely one https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 14/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community of the building blocks of success. If you're having trouble at the hard right edge now it HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT might pay you to go back to the left. I don't know how you think through or solve problems, but it's usually best to identify them, state them, and break them down bite sized chunks to be solved. You will have noticed that this is a major part of how I approach things - it's got me this far, but I really am struggling with the next bit right now . I also don't know what draws your attention when you look at a chart: if several people look at the same chart, they will all see different things! For instance: 1. Do you notice the peaks and troughs as price zigs and zags or do you just notice the general direction that price takes over time? 2. Do you notice horizontal levels where price reacts or do you notice diagonal lines? 3. Do you notice spikes from levels or clusters at levels? Knowing what you automatically key in to might be the first step in working through your stumbling block. When reading others' journals remember that although everyone is pretty much doing the same thing here, they may describe it differently, and each may arrive at the same point by different paths: that is a reflection of those different thought processes we each have. You might find one person's way confusing because you just don't see things their way and you might gel with another's because you do! It might be that as your experience and knowledge grow the first person's way becomes more obvious! There is no right or wrong to this (all views are valid if they are long-term profitable) you just have to find and stick with what works for you and grow from there - another of those building blocks for success. If you don't know what you key in to try what I did way way back. I took charts and drew 'random' lines on them (knowing I would necessarily apply some element of judgment: brains aren't good at random, they are pattern seekers). I looked at what converged on those lines. I rinsed and repeated this several times until I confirmed that I was generally identifying the same things each time. Then I built from that. Hopefully there's something of use here, but good luck and good hunting. This is my take on it so it probably isn't "according to Hoyle", keep your pinches of salt to hand Compression usually happens at the end of a Momentum Move (or some sort of main move). Buyers or sellers have thrust price - unopposed - in their chosen direction https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 25/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community (either because they had the strength to overwhelm the opposition or the opposition HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT was sorely lacking (each can have different implications)). Eventually they reach an area where opposition slowly increases - the Momentum Move is over but inertia (read late buying or selling) sees price continue in the same direction. The strength is slowly sapped out of this incursive move until there is none left and the opposition can now push price in their direction... . Compression should be self-defeating, that's why it's thought of as a leading 'indicator' as price approaches Supply or Demand. What you see is the law of diminishing returns in effect: each successive thrust depletes orders to support the move, sees less distance covered for the same effort, and drives price deeper into opposition, perhaps even convinces opposition they are witnessing increasing weakness so attracting more of the same. The very shape (wedge-like) expresses Compression! But it is not really Compression (in the sense it is meant in RTM) until price can close within its confines - that is, we do not know whether it is consumption of orders or rejection of 'cheap' prices, until we know . That is, price could just as easily 'compress' into Supply or Demand and then drive straight through it (there are many variables at play here). Also, that price is likely to be turned back at the last place of strength (i.e. the transition from Momo to Compression) implies the beginning of ranging and perhaps a continuation of price in the same direction as Momo, but as with all things it doesn't have to be this way. Compression examples (please note that a little poetic licence was used ): Warning: Spoiler! [ Click to expand ] What you were seeing on EURUSD is a Flag. There are some similarities and many differences between a Flag and Compression: 1. Both occur at the end of Momo (or some form of main move) https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 26/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community 2. Compression continues with the Momo whereas the Flag goes against it, HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT because... 3. Compression occurs going into opposition Supply or Demand whereas a Flag occurs after hitting it 4. This also means that when Compression eventually hits Supply or Demand it is still fresh whereas the Flag has already started depleting it, so... 5. On retracing through Compression price is likely to be turned back at the last place where strength was shown whereas once a Flag has completed its pull back (to a source from which the Momo or main move can continue) it is likely to drive through its own extreme and continue with the main move 6. But, the transition from Momo to Compression is a base of strength which might lead to support ranging, and that might lead to a continuation of the main move, so in the end the outcome might be the same for both Compression and Flag even though the mechanism is different 7. Both deplete orders within their structures, removing obstacles to any move through them 8. Oh, and Compression is generally wedge-shaped whereas Flags form equidistant channels EURUSD Flag: Warning: Spoiler! [ Click to expand ] Remember: learner driver, so caveat lector! But I hope this helps all the same. Regards. Spongy. Mine is my way based on what I saw in the Compression Homework section when I first visited, reinforced by my many visits to Redsword11's PIE forum on Forex Factory (950 pages and still going...). I haven't been into the Compression Homework section for a long while but I noticed there is now a PDF at its start, it's instructional :) Compression.pdf There are several examples that agree with your definition, but there's one specific one (on page 19, showing both structures on one chart, but you might want to start reading the text from page 16 as well) which highlights all the difference for me - it's from Redsword11. https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 31/38 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community As I say, there are several similarities between Compression and Flags, not least that HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT as each rises to Supply (or falls to Demand) it absorbs buy (sell) orders, creating weakness within its structure, allowing price to move through it with relative ease (if that is the direction that price then takes). But there are important differences which prepare us to anticipate different Price Action after we see them: after Compression price might range in a PAZ before making its next move (possibly continuing in the same direction as the main move); after a Flag price might just shoot through and continue on in what's called a measured move (the first 'CP' example in your chart above shows this, so does Redsword11's example on page 19). It's a shame that Redsword11's example doesn't capture what happened after the CP, but there are other examples from page 20 onwards which not only blur the definition of Compression but show price ranging after it. None of these things is set in concrete, and anything can and will happen - it's all about the next candle! And, of course, reference time-frame is important, so while your H1 example shows two Flags (as I would call them), they are part of a larger structure on H4 and D1 (in this case a range). It's sometimes difficult to decide what you're seeing and what you should pay most attention to... . In the end, as long as you know what you mean by Compression, and what you anticipate could happen after, that's what matters most. :) Putting all that to one side, I'm not entirely sure I understand what you mean when you say your "main challenge is very often what looks like compression to me on the HTF doesn't translate to compression in the LTF". If I've understood the challenge correctly (and I'm probably very wide of the mark), then if you see Compression on some HTF (spiking into Demand as price rises, for instance), all you do on LTF is check whether ever higher Demand areas are being created in the shadows of those HTF spikes and that all these stacked Demands have been tested. There will be a lot of other detail down here, but your focus should be on the structure you saw on HTF. So: Warning: Spoiler! Harry's reply in "about compression" post 851 The following user(s) said Thank You: Michal238 Moving from thinking to doing QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE THANK YOU In2forex Journal #48 Posts: 196 Spongiform Offline Trader 34/38 Thank you received: 273 https://www.readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/journals/1858-in2forex-journal?start=25 7/27/2018 In2forex Journal - Page 2 - RTM Community Hello in2forex, EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT HOME One thing I've learned as time passes, is don't sweat the detail too much (a terrible thing for an engineer to contemplate, let alone say). Price Action is rarely going to be perfect, so if you have a directional bias and you see a road block, that will be your TP1. If you can't get good R : R to this from your chosen opportunity to enter, then it's failed your quality filters - drop it and move on. The occasional fresh Demand is an occupational hazard of Compression, and we don't know whether it will be respected or disregarded when price gets there. For the EURUSD example - if you could have found an area to get in with good R : R - that fresh Demand level was an obvious point for TP1. When I saw that particular Demand area print I thought it would be a great area to get long at (which is why it was an obvious TP1 if you were looking to short), but because it was in a Flag I was only thinking of shorts. When I step back and take in the D1, that whole structure is not much in the grand scheme of things - of course it was going to go up... . Regards. Spongy.
